If the scale goes up even a little bit, even just a fraction, I get this sudden sharp panic, like I’m losing hold of the discipline I’ve worked quite hard to keep. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, there are times I feel I’ve eaten more than I intended, and then I feel quite uncomfortable about it, but not like a large amount in a short time where I couldn’t stop. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that phrase sounds. I can feel anxious if I’ve had more than I planned, and then I’ll sort of mentally review it, but I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing. It’s not like I suddenly come to and realize I couldn’t stop. I hope that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… occupational health suggested I come in, really. I think they’re a bit concerned because I’ve got into a habit of sort of pushing breakfast and lunch aside when I’m busy with lab runs or exhibition work. And then when I do eat later, I tend to keep it quite small and… controlled, I suppose. It doesn’t feel dramatic to me, but they thought it would be sensible to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my clothes are definitely looser, and when I’ve checked, the numbers have sort of kept drifting down. I wouldn’t say I’ve been trying to make it a big thing, though. Part of me sees it as… being focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it quite as wanting to be thinner. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? It’s more that when I look in the mirror before going into the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not very serious. I know that probably sounds a bit odd.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but… yes, I suppose I am. If the scale goes up even a little bit, even just a fraction, I get this sudden sharp panic, like I’m losing hold of the discipline I’ve worked quite hard to keep. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, there are times I feel I’ve eaten more than I intended, and then I feel quite uncomfortable about it, but not like a large amount in a short time where I couldn’t stop. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that phrase sounds. I can feel anxious if I’ve had more than I planned, and then I’ll sort of mentally review it, but I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing. It’s not like I suddenly come to and realize I couldn’t stop. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might lose control over what or how much you eat at some point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that way. I think I worry more about… staying careful, if that makes sense. I don’t usually feel like I’m on the verge of losing control, but I do like to know what I’m doing and not be surprised by it. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is controlled by a fear of losing control over your food intake?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not exactly a fear of losing control, no. It’s more that I’ve got into a pattern, especially with lab runs or exhibition work, where I push breakfast and lunch aside, and then when I do eat I keep it very small and controlled. Occupational health seemed concerned about that, I think. I don’t mean it to sound dramatic—it just feels easier to keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner would make you feel more in control or successful in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose part of me does think that, yes, though I don’t like admitting it. Before I go into the lab, if I catch myself in the mirror, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and then I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not quite serious enough. Which sounds a bit irrational when I say it out loud. But yes, I think there’s a link there for me, even if I try to frame it as just wanting to look professional.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you thought was excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I might feel uncomfortable after eating more than I planned, but it wouldn’t be what most people would call a large amount, probably. It’s more that it feels excessive to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself sick, and I don’t use laxatives after eating. I mean, I know that’s something people worry about, but that isn’t something I do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Not always, exactly… I mean, I can work and have conversations and seem normal. But it’s there a lot more than I’d like to admit. If I weigh myself and the number has gone up even a tiny bit, I get this really sharp panic, like I’m losing control or losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to keep. I know that probably sounds intense… but yes, it takes up a lot of space in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’m still doing my work and meeting the main deadlines, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals around gallery planning, things like that. I tell myself it’s just easier or I’m busy, but I think it has made me more separate from colleagues than I used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your eating habits or concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I’ve tried to tell myself it’s just because I’m busy. I’m still meeting my main deadlines at work, so it hasn’t… completely interfered, I guess. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals during gallery planning. It’s easier to avoid them, and I think that’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… occupational health suggested I come in, really. I think they’re a bit concerned because I’ve got into a habit of sort of pushing breakfast and lunch aside when I’m busy with lab runs or exhibition work. And then when I do eat later, I tend to keep it quite small and… controlled, I suppose. It doesn’t feel dramatic to me, but they thought it would be sensible to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my clothes are definitely looser, and when I’ve checked, the numbers have sort of kept drifting down. I wouldn’t say I’ve been trying to make it a big thing, though. Part of me sees it as… being focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it quite as wanting to be thinner. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? It’s more that when I look in the mirror before going into the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not very serious. I know that probably sounds a bit odd.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but… yes, I suppose I am. If the scale goes up even a little bit, even just a fraction, I get this sudden sharp panic, like I’m losing hold of the discipline I’ve worked quite hard to keep. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, there are times I feel I’ve eaten more than I intended, and then I feel quite uncomfortable about it, but not like a large amount in a short time where I couldn’t stop. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that phrase sounds. I can feel anxious if I’ve had more than I planned, and then I’ll sort of mentally review it, but I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing. It’s not like I suddenly come to and realize I couldn’t stop. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might lose control over what or how much you eat at some point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that way. I think I worry more about… staying careful, if that makes sense. I don’t usually feel like I’m on the verge of losing control, but I do like to know what I’m doing and not be surprised by it. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is controlled by a fear of losing control over your food intake?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not exactly a fear of losing control, no. It’s more that I’ve got into a pattern, especially with lab runs or exhibition work, where I push breakfast and lunch aside, and then when I do eat I keep it very small and controlled. Occupational health seemed concerned about that, I think. I don’t mean it to sound dramatic—it just feels easier to keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner would make you feel more in control or successful in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose part of me does think that, yes, though I don’t like admitting it. Before I go into the lab, if I catch myself in the mirror, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and then I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not quite serious enough. Which sounds a bit irrational when I say it out loud. But yes, I think there’s a link there for me, even if I try to frame it as just wanting to look professional.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you thought was excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I might feel uncomfortable after eating more than I planned, but it wouldn’t be what most people would call a large amount, probably. It’s more that it feels excessive to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself sick, and I don’t use laxatives after eating. I mean, I know that’s something people worry about, but that isn’t something I do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Not always, exactly… I mean, I can work and have conversations and seem normal. I know that probably sounds intense… but yes, it takes up a lot of space in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’m still doing my work and meeting the main deadlines, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals around gallery planning, things like that. I tell myself it’s just easier or I’m busy, but I think it has made me more separate from colleagues than I used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your eating habits or concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I’ve tried to tell myself it’s just because I’m busy. I’m still meeting my main deadlines at work, so it hasn’t… completely interfered, I guess. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals during gallery planning. It’s easier to avoid them, and I think that’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your weight loss has been significant enough that others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I haven’t asked people directly, but my clothes are definitely looser, and the numbers on the scale have kept drifting down. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, exactly. Part of me has been seeing it as… proof that I’m staying focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not a number exactly… or I tell myself it isn’t. It’s more that, when I look in the mirror before going to the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I wouldn’t say I’ve been trying to make it a big thing, though. Part of me sees it as… being focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it quite as wanting to be thinner. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? It’s more that when I look in the mirror before going into the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not very serious. I know that probably sounds a bit odd.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but… yes, I suppose I am. If the scale goes up even a little bit, even just a fraction, I get this sudden sharp panic, like I’m losing hold of the discipline I’ve worked quite hard to keep. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, there are times I feel I’ve eaten more than I intended, and then I feel quite uncomfortable about it, but not like a large amount in a short time where I couldn’t stop. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that phrase sounds. I can feel anxious if I’ve had more than I planned, and then I’ll sort of mentally review it, but I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing. It’s not like I suddenly come to and realize I couldn’t stop. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might lose control over what or how much you eat at some point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that way. I think I worry more about… staying careful, if that makes sense. I don’t usually feel like I’m on the verge of losing control, but I do like to know what I’m doing and not be surprised by it. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is controlled by a fear of losing control over your food intake?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not exactly a fear of losing control, no. It’s more that I’ve got into a pattern, especially with lab runs or exhibition work, where I push breakfast and lunch aside, and then when I do eat I keep it very small and controlled. Occupational health seemed concerned about that, I think. I don’t mean it to sound dramatic—it just feels easier to keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner would make you feel more in control or successful in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose part of me does think that, yes, though I don’t like admitting it. Before I go into the lab, if I catch myself in the mirror, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and then I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not quite serious enough. Which sounds a bit irrational when I say it out loud. But yes, I think there’s a link there for me, even if I try to frame it as just wanting to look professional.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you thought was excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I might feel uncomfortable after eating more than I planned, but it wouldn’t be what most people would call a large amount, probably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your eating habits or concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I’ve tried to tell myself it’s just because I’m busy. I wouldn’t say I’ve been trying to make it a big thing, though. Part of me sees it as… being focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it quite as wanting to be thinner. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? It’s more that when I look in the mirror before going into the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not very serious. I know that probably sounds a bit odd.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but… yes, I suppose I am. If the scale goes up even a little bit, even just a fraction, I get this sudden sharp panic, like I’m losing hold of the discipline I’ve worked quite hard to keep. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, there are times I feel I’ve eaten more than I intended, and then I feel quite uncomfortable about it, but not like a large amount in a short time where I couldn’t stop. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that phrase sounds. I can feel anxious if I’ve had more than I planned, and then I’ll sort of mentally review it, but I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing. It’s not like I suddenly come to and realize I couldn’t stop. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might lose control over what or how much you eat at some point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that way. I think I worry more about… staying careful, if that makes sense. I don’t usually feel like I’m on the verge of losing control, but I do like to know what I’m doing and not be surprised by it. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is controlled by a fear of losing control over your food intake?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not exactly a fear of losing control, no. It’s more that I’ve got into a pattern, especially with lab runs or exhibition work, where I push breakfast and lunch aside, and then when I do eat I keep it very small and controlled. Occupational health seemed concerned about that, I think. I don’t mean it to sound dramatic—it just feels easier to keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner would make you feel more in control or successful in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose part of me does think that, yes, though I don’t like admitting it. Before I go into the lab, if I catch myself in the mirror, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and then I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not quite serious enough. Which sounds a bit irrational when I say it out loud. But yes, I think there’s a link there for me, even if I try to frame it as just wanting to look professional.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you thought was excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I might feel uncomfortable after eating more than I planned, but it wouldn’t be what most people would call a large amount, probably. It’s more that it feels excessive to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself sick, and I don’t use laxatives after eating. I mean, I know that’s something people worry about, but that isn’t something I do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Not always, exactly… I mean, I can work and have conversations and seem normal. But it’s there a lot more than I’d like to admit. If I weigh myself and the number has gone up even a tiny bit, I get this really sharp panic, like I’m losing control or losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to keep. I know that probably sounds intense… but yes, it takes up a lot of space in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’m still doing my work and meeting the main deadlines, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals around gallery planning, things like that. I tell myself it’s just easier or I’m busy, but I think it has made me more separate from colleagues than I used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your eating habits or concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I’ve tried to tell myself it’s just because I’m busy. I’m still meeting my main deadlines at work, so it hasn’t… completely interfered, I guess. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals during gallery planning. It’s easier to avoid them, and I think that’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your weight loss has been significant enough that others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I haven’t asked people directly, but my clothes are definitely looser, and the numbers on the scale have kept drifting down. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, exactly. Part of me has been seeing it as… proof that I’m staying focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight or body shape in mind that you are trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: Not a number exactly… or I tell myself it isn’t. It’s more that, when I look in the mirror before going to the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I notice my stomach or my hips first, and they just… make me feel like I look careless, or not serious enough. I know that may sound a bit irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult—I think that’s just where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you eat normally, followed by periods of very restricted eating?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “normally,” I don’t think… I mean, I suppose occupational health sent me because it’s become a bit of a pattern. During lab runs or exhibition work, I’ll push breakfast and lunch aside, telling myself I’ll eat later. And then when I do, I tend to keep it very small and… controlled.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as periods of normal eating versus restricted eating. It’s more that the restricted part has become the default on busy days. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were at a weight that you considered acceptable, you would be able to eat more freely without restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, yes… I mean, if I felt okay with my weight, maybe I could be less rigid. But honestly, the pattern has become a bit automatic. During lab runs or exhibition work I tend to push breakfast and lunch aside, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled.\n\nI’m not trying to be extreme about it—it just feels safer that way, I suppose. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It doesn’t feel dramatic to me, but they thought it would be sensible to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my clothes are definitely looser, and when I’ve checked, the numbers have sort of kept drifting down. I wouldn’t say I’ve been trying to make it a big thing, though. Part of me sees it as… being focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it quite as wanting to be thinner. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? It’s more that when I look in the mirror before going into the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not very serious. I know that probably sounds a bit odd.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but… yes, I suppose I am. If the scale goes up even a little bit, even just a fraction, I get this sudden sharp panic, like I’m losing hold of the discipline I’ve worked quite hard to keep. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, there are times I feel I’ve eaten more than I intended, and then I feel quite uncomfortable about it, but not like a large amount in a short time where I couldn’t stop. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that phrase sounds. I can feel anxious if I’ve had more than I planned, and then I’ll sort of mentally review it, but I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing. It’s not like I suddenly come to and realize I couldn’t stop. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might lose control over what or how much you eat at some point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that way. I think I worry more about… staying careful, if that makes sense. I don’t usually feel like I’m on the verge of losing control, but I do like to know what I’m doing and not be surprised by it. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is controlled by a fear of losing control over your food intake?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not exactly a fear of losing control, no. It’s more that I’ve got into a pattern, especially with lab runs or exhibition work, where I push breakfast and lunch aside, and then when I do eat I keep it very small and controlled. Occupational health seemed concerned about that, I think. I don’t mean it to sound dramatic—it just feels easier to keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner would make you feel more in control or successful in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose part of me does think that, yes, though I don’t like admitting it. Before I go into the lab, if I catch myself in the mirror, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and then I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not quite serious enough. Which sounds a bit irrational when I say it out loud. But yes, I think there’s a link there for me, even if I try to frame it as just wanting to look professional.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you thought was excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I might feel uncomfortable after eating more than I planned, but it wouldn’t be what most people would call a large amount, probably. It’s more that it feels excessive to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself sick, and I don’t use laxatives after eating. I mean, I know that’s something people worry about, but that isn’t something I do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Not always, exactly… I mean, I can work and have conversations and seem normal. But it’s there a lot more than I’d like to admit. If I weigh myself and the number has gone up even a tiny bit, I get this really sharp panic, like I’m losing control or losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to keep. I know that probably sounds intense… but yes, it takes up a lot of space in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’m still doing my work and meeting the main deadlines, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals around gallery planning, things like that. I tell myself it’s just easier or I’m busy, but I think it has made me more separate from colleagues than I used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your eating habits or concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I’ve tried to tell myself it’s just because I’m busy. I’m still meeting my main deadlines at work, so it hasn’t… completely interfered, I guess. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals during gallery planning. It’s easier to avoid them, and I think that’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your weight loss has been significant enough that others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I haven’t asked people directly, but my clothes are definitely looser, and the numbers on the scale have kept drifting down. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, exactly. Part of me has been seeing it as… proof that I’m staying focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight or body shape in mind that you are trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: Not a number exactly… or I tell myself it isn’t. It’s more that, when I look in the mirror before going to the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I notice my stomach or my hips first, and they just… make me feel like I look careless, or not serious enough. I know that may sound a bit irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult—I think that’s just where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you eat normally, followed by periods of very restricted eating?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “normally,” I don’t think… I mean, I suppose occupational health sent me because it’s become a bit of a pattern. During lab runs or exhibition work, I’ll push breakfast and lunch aside, telling myself I’ll eat later. And then when I do, I tend to keep it very small and… controlled.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as periods of normal eating versus restricted eating. It’s more that the restricted part has become the default on busy days. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were at a weight that you considered acceptable, you would be able to eat more freely without restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, yes… I mean, if I felt okay with my weight, maybe I could be less rigid. But honestly, the pattern has become a bit automatic. During lab runs or exhibition work I tend to push breakfast and lunch aside, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled.\n\nI’m not trying to be extreme about it—it just feels safer that way, I suppose. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to restrict your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is probably stronger than I’d like to admit.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little—just a fraction, really—I feel this sharp panic, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked hard to maintain. I know that sounds a bit rigid. I don’t mean it to be dramatic. It’s just… restricting feels like the way I keep that from happening. Does that answer it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… occupational health suggested I come in, really. I think they’re a bit concerned because I’ve got into a habit of sort of pushing breakfast and lunch aside when I’m busy with lab runs or exhibition work. And then when I do eat later, I tend to keep it quite small and… controlled, I suppose. It doesn’t feel dramatic to me, but they thought it would be sensible to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, my clothes are definitely looser, and when I’ve checked, the numbers have sort of kept drifting down. I wouldn’t say I’ve been trying to make it a big thing, though. Part of me sees it as… being focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you want to be thinner?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d put it quite as wanting to be thinner. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? It’s more that when I look in the mirror before going into the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not very serious. I know that probably sounds a bit odd.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m terrified all the time, but… yes, I suppose I am. If the scale goes up even a little bit, even just a fraction, I get this sudden sharp panic, like I’m losing hold of the discipline I’ve worked quite hard to keep. I know that probably sounds excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, there are times I feel I’ve eaten more than I intended, and then I feel quite uncomfortable about it, but not like a large amount in a short time where I couldn’t stop. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while you're eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that phrase sounds. I can feel anxious if I’ve had more than I planned, and then I’ll sort of mentally review it, but I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing. It’s not like I suddenly come to and realize I couldn’t stop. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might lose control over what or how much you eat at some point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that way. I think I worry more about… staying careful, if that makes sense. I don’t usually feel like I’m on the verge of losing control, but I do like to know what I’m doing and not be surprised by it. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is controlled by a fear of losing control over your food intake?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not exactly a fear of losing control, no. It’s more that I’ve got into a pattern, especially with lab runs or exhibition work, where I push breakfast and lunch aside, and then when I do eat I keep it very small and controlled. Occupational health seemed concerned about that, I think. I don’t mean it to sound dramatic—it just feels easier to keep it contained, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner would make you feel more in control or successful in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose part of me does think that, yes, though I don’t like admitting it. Before I go into the lab, if I catch myself in the mirror, I don’t really see someone thin. I tend to notice my stomach or my hips, and then I get this feeling that they make me look… careless, or not quite serious enough. Which sounds a bit irrational when I say it out loud. But yes, I think there’s a link there for me, even if I try to frame it as just wanting to look professional.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten an amount of food that you thought was excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I might feel uncomfortable after eating more than I planned, but it wouldn’t be what most people would call a large amount, probably. It’s more that it feels excessive to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself sick, and I don’t use laxatives after eating. I mean, I know that’s something people worry about, but that isn’t something I do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Not always, exactly… I mean, I can work and have conversations and seem normal. But it’s there a lot more than I’d like to admit. If I weigh myself and the number has gone up even a tiny bit, I get this really sharp panic, like I’m losing control or losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to keep. I know that probably sounds intense… but yes, it takes up a lot of space in my head.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of gaining weight affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. I’m still doing my work and meeting the main deadlines, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals around gallery planning, things like that. I tell myself it’s just easier or I’m busy, but I think it has made me more separate from colleagues than I used to be. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities because of your eating habits or concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I’ve tried to tell myself it’s just because I’m busy. I’m still meeting my main deadlines at work, so it hasn’t… completely interfered, I guess. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches and those informal meals during gallery planning. It’s easier to avoid them, and I think that’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your weight loss has been significant enough that others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I haven’t asked people directly, but my clothes are definitely looser, and the numbers on the scale have kept drifting down. I don’t mean that in a dramatic way, exactly. Part of me has been seeing it as… proof that I’m staying focused, I suppose, like I’m not letting all the chaos at work spill over into my body. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a specific weight or body shape in mind that you are trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: Not a number exactly… or I tell myself it isn’t. It’s more that, when I look in the mirror before going to the lab, I don’t really see someone thin. I notice my stomach or my hips first, and they just… make me feel like I look careless, or not serious enough. I know that may sound a bit irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult—I think that’s just where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you have periods where you eat normally, followed by periods of very restricted eating?\n\nPatient: Not exactly “normally,” I don’t think… I mean, I suppose occupational health sent me because it’s become a bit of a pattern. During lab runs or exhibition work, I’ll push breakfast and lunch aside, telling myself I’ll eat later. And then when I do, I tend to keep it very small and… controlled.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as periods of normal eating versus restricted eating. It’s more that the restricted part has become the default on busy days. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you were at a weight that you considered acceptable, you would be able to eat more freely without restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, yes… I mean, if I felt okay with my weight, maybe I could be less rigid. But honestly, the pattern has become a bit automatic. During lab runs or exhibition work I tend to push breakfast and lunch aside, and then by the time I do eat, I keep it very small and controlled.\n\nI’m not trying to be extreme about it—it just feels safer that way, I suppose. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to restrict your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but if I’m being honest, the weight part is probably stronger than I’d like to admit.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a little—just a fraction, really—I feel this sharp panic, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked hard to maintain. I know that sounds a bit rigid. I don’t mean it to be dramatic. It’s just… restricting feels like the way I keep that from happening. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about avoiding weight gain or maintaining a sense of control over your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about avoiding weight gain, if I’m being completely honest… though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nWhen the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this really sharp panic, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. I suppose the control part is tied into that, but the fear of gaining is what hits first. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
